(97)

\displaystyle\hphantom{I_{1110011}^{\eta}}=\frac{1}{128x^{2}\omega^{4}}\left[-\frac{1-3\eta}{8\pi^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{1-3\eta}{4\pi^{2}\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{4\omega^{2}x^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\frac{1-3\eta}{48}-\frac{iG(-\eta,x)}{\pi}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{1-3\eta}{4\pi^{2}}\log^{2}\left(\frac{4\omega^{2}x^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\frac{1-2\eta}{\pi^{2}}\left[G(1,0,x)+G(-1,0,x)\right]\right]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).

The numerator of the second of these,
I_{11-11101}^{\eta}
, is symbolized by
\mathcal{N}
in the diagrammatic representation.
Evaluating the iterations in
eq.

21
requires also the complex conjugates of these master integrals.

Omitting powers of
\omega
for brevity, the vector of two-loop master integrals

\displaystyle\vec{I}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{1100000},-I_{0001101},-I_{0110001},I_{1001101},I_{11-11101},-I_{1110011}\end{pmatrix}^{T},

(98)

is canonicalized into

\displaystyle\vec{I}_{\rm c}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{1100000}\\
-\frac{\varepsilon(6\varepsilon-1)}{5(1-2\varepsilon)^{2}}I_{0001101}\\
-\frac{\varepsilon(6\varepsilon-1)}{5(1-2\varepsilon)^{2}}I_{0110001}\\
\frac{\varepsilon^{2}x}{(1-2\varepsilon)^{2}}I_{1001101}\\
\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-2\varepsilon)^{2}}I_{11-11101}\\
-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}x^{2}}{(1-2\varepsilon)^{2}}I_{1110011}\end{pmatrix}

(99)

In particular, the transformation matrix that renders the masters into canonical form is diagonal.

Appendix C
Spin-weighted spherical harmonics

We collect here pertinent conventions and relations relating to spin-weighted spherical harmonics.

Spin-weighted spherical harmonics furnish a basis for functions defined on the sphere.
As such, they are orthonormal,

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}\Omega\,{}_{s^{\prime}}\bar{Y}_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}(\theta,\phi)\,{}_{s}Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=\delta_{ss^{\prime}}\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\,\delta_{mm^{\prime}},

(100)

where
\int{\rm d}\Omega=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin\theta\,{\rm d}\theta\,{\rm d}\phi
.
Any function
f
of
\theta\in[0,\pi],\,\phi\in[0,2\pi)
can be written as

\displaystyle f(\theta,\phi)=\sum_{l=|s|}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}{}_{s}Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)f_{lm}.

(101)

Through the orthonormality property of the spherical harmonics, the modes of the function are determined through

\displaystyle f_{lm}=\int{\rm d}\Omega\,{}_{s}\bar{Y}_{lm}(\theta,\phi)f(\theta,\phi).

(102)

The conjugate harmonic in this equation can be replaced by an unconjugated one through

\displaystyle{}_{s}\bar{Y}_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=(-1)^{s+m}{}_{-s}Y_{l(-m)}(\theta,\phi).

(103)

The dependence of the harmonics on the azimuthal angle is simply a phase,

\displaystyle{}_{s}Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=e^{im\phi}{}_{s}Y_{lm}(\theta,0).

(104)

In the body of the paper, whenever the second argument is omitted, it is assumed to be 0,
{}_{s}Y_{lm}(\theta)\equiv{}_{s}Y_{lm}(\theta,0)
.

The reflection properties of the harmonics are summarized by

\displaystyle{}_{s}Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=(-1)^{l}\,{}_{-s}Y_{lm}(\pi-\theta,\phi+\pi).

(105)

Intuitively, if a unit vector
\boldsymbol{n}
is given by the coordinates
(\theta,\phi)
, then
-\boldsymbol{n}
has the coordinates
(\pi-\theta,\phi+\pi)
.

A final essential property is the addition theorem for spin-weighted spherical harmonics
102
:

\displaystyle(-1)^{s}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}{}_{s_{1}}\bar{Y}_{lm}(\theta_{1},\phi_{1})\,{}_{s_{2}}Y_{lm}(\theta_{2},\phi_{2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2l+1}{4\pi}}e^{-is_{2}\alpha}{}_{s_{2}}Y_{l(-s_{1})}(\beta,\gamma),

(106)

valid for
l\geq\max(|s|,|s^{\prime}|)
and where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
are the Euler angles, which appeared in the main text but which we reproduce here for convenience:

\displaystyle\cot\alpha

\displaystyle=\cos\theta_{2}\cot(\phi_{2}-\phi_{1})-\cot\theta_{1}\sin\theta_{2}\csc(\phi_{2}-\phi_{1})

(107)

\displaystyle\cos\beta

\displaystyle=\cos\theta_{1}\cos\theta_{2}+\sin\theta_{1}\sin\theta_{2}\cos(\phi_{2}-\phi_{1})

(108)

\displaystyle\cot\gamma

\displaystyle=\cos\theta_{1}\cot(\phi_{2}-\phi_{1})-\cot\theta_{2}\sin\theta_{1}\csc(\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}).

(109)

Multiplying both sides by
{}_{s_{1}}Y_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}(\theta_{1},\phi_{1})
with
|m^{\prime}|\leq\min(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
and integrating over the solid angle
{\rm d}\Omega_{1}
of
\theta_{1},\phi_{1}
:

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}\Omega_{1}\,e^{-is_{2}\alpha}{}_{s_{2}}Y_{l(-s_{1})}(\beta,\gamma)\,{}_{s_{1}}Y_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}(\theta_{1},\phi_{1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2l+1}}\,{}_{s_{2}}Y_{lm^{\prime}}(\theta_{2},\phi_{2})\delta_{ll^{\prime}}.

(110)

The Euler angles appearing here have intuitive physical interpretations not apparent from
eqs.

107
,
108
and
109
.
Understanding them is central to understanding the general form in
eq.

33
.
Let us explore them further now.

C.1
Euler angles

Consider two unit vectors
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}=\boldsymbol{n}_{1}(\theta_{1},\phi_{1})
and
\boldsymbol{n}_{2}=\boldsymbol{n}_{2}(\theta_{2},\phi_{2})
.
The angle between these vectors is easily shown to be
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{2}=\cos\beta
.

Now, the vector
\boldsymbol{n}_{i}
induces a coordinate system
(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{x},\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{y},\boldsymbol{n}_{i})
, with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}}\boldsymbol{n}_{1},\quad\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{y}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{1}}\boldsymbol{n}_{1},

(111)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{2}^{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{2}}\boldsymbol{n}_{2},\quad\boldsymbol{e}_{2}^{y}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{2}}\boldsymbol{n}_{2}.

(112)

With this construction, the remaining Euler angles represent the azimuthal angle of
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}
(
\boldsymbol{n}_{2}
) in the frame induced by
\boldsymbol{n}_{2}
(
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}
).
Specifically

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{2}^{x}=\cos\alpha,

\displaystyle\quad\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{2}^{y}=-\sin\alpha,

(113)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{n}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{x}=\cos\gamma,

\displaystyle\quad\boldsymbol{n}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{y}=\sin\gamma.

(114)

Changing to the circular basis,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{\sigma}=\frac{\boldsymbol{e}^{x}+i\sigma\boldsymbol{e}^{y}}{\sqrt{2}},

(115)

for
\sigma=\pm 1
, one finds that these two angles are nothing but little group phases in an amplitude consisting of these polarizations and the
\boldsymbol{n}_{i}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}^{\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{-i\sigma\alpha},\quad\boldsymbol{n}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{i\sigma\gamma},\quad\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{e^{i(\sigma_{1}\gamma-\sigma_{2}\alpha)}}{\cos\beta+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}.

(116)

The only non-trivial angular dependence of such an amplitude is therefore on
\beta
.
Note that the poles in
\beta
of the last dot product are spurious, and disappear when this product is expressed in terms of the original angles.
